=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N) of length N and a positive integer K (at most N).
For each i = 1, 2, \dots, N, solve the following problem:

- When you choose K elements from A that include A_i, find the maximum possible GCD (greatest common divisor) of those chosen elements.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print N lines. The j-th line should contain the answer for i=j.

Constraints


- 1 \leq K \leq N \leq 1.2 \times 10^6
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^6
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 2
3 4 6 7 12

Sample Output 1

3
4
6
1
6

For i=1, choosing A_1 and A_3 yields \gcd(\lbrace 3,6 \rbrace) = 3, which is the maximum.
For i=2, choosing A_2 and A_5 yields \gcd(\lbrace 4,12 \rbrace) = 4, which is the maximum.
For i=3, choosing A_3 and A_5 yields \gcd(\lbrace 6,12 \rbrace) = 6, which is the maximum.
For i=4, choosing A_4 and A_2 yields \gcd(\lbrace 7,4 \rbrace) = 1, which is the maximum.
For i=5, choosing A_5 and A_3 yields \gcd(\lbrace 12,6 \rbrace) = 6, which is the maximum.

Sample Input 2

3 3
6 10 15

Sample Output 2

1
1
1

Sample Input 3

10 3
414003 854320 485570 52740 833292 625990 909680 885153 435420 221663

Sample Output 3

59
590
590
879
879
590
20
879
590
59

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Custom parser implementation details:

```
import sys
from array import array

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    L = len(data)
    idx = 0
    # skip whitespace
    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    N = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        N = N*10 + (data[idx]-48)
        idx += 1
    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    K = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        K = K*10 + (data[idx]-48)
        idx += 1
    A = array('I')
    # maybe preallocate? array doesn't have reserve. Could use list? 
```
Appending to array 1.2e6 okay. But array append may reallocate; fine.

Need parse N values. We can loop:
```

A = array('I')
for _ in range(N):
    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    v = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        v = v*10 + (data[idx]-48)
        idx += 1
    A.append(v)
    if v > max_val: max_val = v
```
This avoids generator overhead? It's manual but code repeated. Need ensure idx < L in while conditions. Input well-formed. At end maybe no trailing whitespace; while data[idx] > 32 needs idx < L. Use `while idx < L and data[idx] > 32`. Good.

Now, let's think if there is a more subtle mathematical issue: We assumed answer = max divisor d of A_i with count_multiples(d)>=K. We proved. Let's re-evaluate with exact gcd. Let's write parser carefully:
```
def main():
    import sys
    from array import array
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    L = len(data)
    idx = 0

    # read N
    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    N = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        N = N * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
        idx += 1

    # read K
    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    K = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        K = K * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
        idx += 1

    A = array('I')
    max_val = 0
    for _ in range(N):
        while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
            idx += 1
        v = 0
        while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
            v = v * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
            idx += 1
        A.append(v)
        if v > max_val:
            max_val = v
```
Note: `data[idx] > 32` works for digits; whitespace includes newline, space. No negative. Good.

Potential speed: The inner while for reading digits uses `data[idx] > 32`; if idx==L, data[idx] would error, but condition `idx < L and ...` short-circuits. Good.

After reading, we can `del data` to free input buffer before heavy loops? Yes, after parsing, data no longer needed. Do `del data` to reduce memory. Good.

Code:

```
import sys
from array import array

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    L = len(data)
    idx = 0

    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    N = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        N = N * 10 + data[idx] - 48
        idx += 1

    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    K = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        K = K * 10 + data[idx] - 48
        idx += 1

    A = array('I')
    max_val = 0
    for _ in range(N):
        while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
            idx += 1
        v = 0
        while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
            v = v * 10 + data[idx] - 48
            idx += 1
        A.append(v)
        if v > max_val:
            max_val = v

    del data

    freq = [0] * (max_val + 1)
    for v in A:
        freq[v] += 1

    ans = [1] * (max_val + 1)
    maxv = max_val
    K_local = K
    freq_local = freq
    ans_local = ans

    for d in range(1, maxv + 1):
        s = 0
        for m in range(d, maxv + 1, d):
            s += freq_local[m]
            if s >= K_local:
                for m2 in range(d, maxv + 1, d):
                    ans_local[m2] = d
                break

    del freq

    out = []
    write = sys.stdout.write
    ans_local = ans
    for v in A:
        out.append(str(ans_local[v]))
        if len(out) >= 10000:
            write('\n'.join(out))
            write('\n')
            out.clear()
    if out:
        write('\n'.join(out))
        write('\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

But memory. N=1.2e6, `split` list of bytes memory maybe high but maybe okay. If memory limit 1024 MB, use `split` for speed. The prompt doesn't specify memory. Many competitive programming solutions in Python use `map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` for 1.2e6 ints; memory maybe ~100MB but okay if limit 1024. But if limit 256, okay? Let's estimate with split and array A:
- data bytes 10MB.
- split list: 1.2e6 bytes objects. Each bytes object overhead ~33 bytes + data (avg 6) =39 bytes -> 46.8MB. List pointers 9.6MB. Total ~56MB.
- If we do `it = map(int, data.split())`, the split list is stored inside the list iterator? Actually `data.split()` returns a list; `map(int, list)` holds an iterator over the list. The list remains until map exhausted. During parsing, we also allocate A array 4.8MB. After parsing, `it` and split list freed? If we delete. Peak during parsing: data 10 + split list 56 + A 4.8 + maybe ints? map yields ints one by one, not stored except A. So ~71MB. Then after parsing, split freed, data maybe still if not del. Then freq 8, ans 36. Total ~50MB. Actually split peak 71, later 50. Under 128. If using list A instead of array, peak split 71 + A int objects 43 =114, maybe close. With array, okay. So `split` might be faster and memory still okay. Should we use `split`? Let's consider code simplicity:
```
import sys
from array import array
it = map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
N = next(it); K = next(it)
A = array('I', [0]) * N
max_val = 0
for i in range(N):
    v = next(it)
    A[i] = v
    if v > max_val: max_val = v
```
But `map(int, data.split())` creates split list of bytes; after `it` exhausted, the list may be freed? The map object holds the list iterator, which holds reference to list until map is exhausted. After the for loop, `it` still exists but exhausted; does it release the list? A list_iterator holds reference to the list even when exhausted? I think yes, until iterator is deleted. So we should `del it` after parsing to free split list. Also `data` is not stored separately if we chain? `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` reads bytes then split; the bytes object is temporary and freed after split returns? Actually `read()` returns bytes, `.split()` is called on it, returns list of bytes; the original bytes object may be freed immediately after split because no reference. So data not retained. Good. So peak: split list 56 + A 4.8 =61. Then `del it` frees split list. This is simpler and likely faster. But `map(int, split_list)` converts each bytes to int in C? `int()` is C but called per element. It's faster than manual Python digit loop. Use this? The memory of split list 56MB is okay. But if memory limit 64MB, split + A + freq + ans might exceed. However, split list is freed before freq/ans allocation? We parse A, then `del it` (and split list freed), then allocate freq and ans. So peak during parsing ~61MB. Later freq+ans+A ~8+36+4.8=48.8MB. Total peak maybe 61MB. If memory limit 64MB, close but maybe okay? Plus interpreter overhead, not. But memory limit likely >64. Custom parser peak lower. Which is safer? The prompt doesn't specify memory. I'd prefer custom parser to be safe memory-wise. But time? Let's think.

Manual parser code is not too complex. It avoids split memory. Time maybe acceptable. The main loops dominate. I'll keep custom parser.

with final code. We can include code block. The code should be complete. Let's ensure no syntax errors.

Let's write final code carefully:

```
import sys
from array import array

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read()
    L = len(data)
    idx = 0

    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    N = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        N = N * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
        idx += 1

    while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
        idx += 1
    K = 0
    while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
        K = K * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
        idx += 1

    A = array('I', [0]) * N
    max_val = 0
    for i in range(N):
        while idx < L and data[idx] <= 32:
            idx += 1
        v = 0
        while idx < L and data[idx] > 32:
            v = v * 10 + (data[idx] - 48)
            idx += 1
        A[i] = v
        if v > max_val:
            max_val = v

    del data

    freq = [0] * (max_val + 1)
    for v in A:
        freq[v] += 1

    ans = [1] * (max_val + 1)
    limit = max_val + 1

    for d in range(2, limit):
        s = 0
        for m in range(d, limit, d):
            s += freq[m]
            if s >= K:
                for m2 in range(d, limit, d):
                    ans[m2] = d
                break

    del freq

    out = []
    write = sys.stdout.write
    for v in A:
        out.append(str(ans[v]))
        if len(out) >= 10000:
            write('\n'.join(out))
            write('\n')
            out.clear()
    if out:
        write('\n'.join(out))
        write('\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
